<p><b>Mali</b><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ɑː/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;father&#39;">ɑː</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Mali.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8a/En-us-Mali.ogg" class="internal" title="En-us-Mali.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span>; <small>French pronunciation:&#160;&#8203;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">[mali]</a></span>), officially the <b>Republic of Mali</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> is a <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">landlocked country</a> in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>. Mali is the eighth-largest country in Africa, with an area of over 1,240,000 square kilometres (480,000&#160;sq&#160;mi). The population of Mali is 21.9 million.<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-13">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-14">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> 67% of its population was estimated to be under the age of 25 in 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Its <a href="/wiki/Capital_city" title="Capital city">capital</a> and largest city is <a href="/wiki/Bamako" title="Bamako">Bamako</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Sovereign_state" title="Sovereign state">sovereign state</a> of Mali consists of ten regions and its borders on the north reach deep into the middle of the <a href="/wiki/Sahara" title="Sahara">Sahara Desert</a>. The country's southern part is in the <a href="/wiki/Sudanian_savanna" title="Sudanian savanna">Sudanian savanna</a>, where the majority of inhabitants live, and both the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger</a> and <a href="/wiki/Senegal_River" title="Senegal River">Senegal</a> rivers pass through. The country's economy centres on agriculture and mining. One of Mali's most prominent natural resources is gold, and the country is the third largest producer of <a href="/wiki/Gold" title="Gold">gold</a> on the African continent.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> It also exports <a href="/wiki/Salt" title="Salt">salt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Present-day Mali was once part of three extremely powerful and wealthy West <a href="/wiki/African_empires" title="African empires">African empires</a> that controlled <a href="/wiki/Trans-Saharan_trade" title="Trans-Saharan trade">trans-Saharan trade</a>: the <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Empire" title="Ghana Empire">Ghana Empire</a> (for which <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> is named), the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> (for which Mali is named), and the <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a>. At its peak in 1300, the Mali Empire was the wealthiest country in Africa, covering an area about twice the size of modern-day France and stretched to the west coast of the continent.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Mali was also one of the wealthiest countries on earth, and its emperor at its zenith, <a href="/wiki/Mansa_Musa" title="Mansa Musa">Mansa Musa</a>, is believed to be possibly the <a href="/wiki/List_of_wealthiest_historical_figures" title="List of wealthiest historical figures">wealthiest individual in history</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Besides being an economic powerhouse, medieval Mali was a centre of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, culture and knowledge, with <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a> becoming a renowned place of learning with its <a href="/wiki/University_of_Timbuktu" title="University of Timbuktu">university</a>, one of the oldest in the world still active. The expanding <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a> absorbed the empire in 1468, followed by a <a href="/wiki/Saadi_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Saadi dynasty">Moroccan</a> army <a href="/wiki/Saadian_invasion_of_the_Songhai_Empire" title="Saadian invasion of the Songhai Empire">which defeated the Songhai in 1591</a>. The Moroccan colonists established a new ruling class, the <a href="/wiki/Arma_people" title="Arma people">Arma</a>, who after 1631 became virtually independent of Morocco.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In the late 19th century, during the <a href="/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa" title="Scramble for Africa">Scramble for Africa</a>, France seized control of Mali, making it a part of <a href="/wiki/French_Sudan" title="French Sudan">French Sudan</a>. French Sudan (then known as the Sudanese Republic) joined with <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> in 1959, achieving independence in 1960 as the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Federation" title="Mali Federation">Mali Federation</a>. Shortly thereafter, following Senegal's withdrawal from the federation, the Sudanese Republic declared itself the independent Republic of Mali. After a long period of one-party rule, a coup in 1991 led to the writing of a new constitution and the establishment of Mali as a democratic, multi-party state.
</p><p>In January 2012, an <a href="/wiki/Northern_Mali_conflict_(2012%E2%80%93present)" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Mali conflict (2012–present)">armed conflict broke out in northern Mali</a>, in which <a href="/wiki/National_Movement_for_the_Liberation_of_Azawad" title="National Movement for the Liberation of Azawad">Tuareg rebels</a> took control of a territory in the north, and in April declared the <a href="/wiki/Secession" title="Secession">secession</a> of a new state, <a href="/wiki/Azawad" title="Azawad">Azawad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The conflict was complicated by a <a href="/wiki/2012_Malian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2012 Malian coup d&#39;état">military coup</a> that took place in March<sup id="cite_ref-telegraph.co.uk_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-telegraph.co.uk-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and later fighting between Tuareg and other rebel factions. In response to territorial gains, the French military launched <a href="/wiki/Operation_Serval" title="Operation Serval">Operation Serval</a> in January 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> A month later, Malian and French forces recaptured most of the north, although the conflict still continued. <a href="/wiki/Malian_presidential_election,_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Malian presidential election, 2013">Presidential elections</a> were held on 28 July 2013, with a second-round run-off held on 11 August, and <a href="/wiki/Malian_parliamentary_election,_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Malian parliamentary election, 2013">legislative elections</a> were held on 24 November and 15 December 2013.
</p><p>In the early 2020s Mali experienced two military takeovers by <a href="/wiki/Assimi_Go%C3%AFta" title="Assimi Goïta">Assimi Goïta</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The name <i>Mali</i> is taken from the&#160;name of the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a>. The name means "the place where the king lives"<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> and carries a connotation of strength.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Guinean writer <a href="/wiki/Djibril_Tamsir_Niane" title="Djibril Tamsir Niane">Djibril Niane</a> suggests in <i>Sundiata: An Epic of Old Mali</i> (1965) that it is not impossible that Mali was the name given to one of the capitals of the emperors. 14th-century Moroccan traveler <a href="/wiki/Ibn_Battuta" title="Ibn Battuta">Ibn Battuta</a> reported that the capital of the Mali Empire was called Mali.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> One <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Mandinka</a> tradition tells that the legendary first emperor <a href="/wiki/Sundiata_Keita" title="Sundiata Keita">Sundiata Keita</a> changed himself into a hippopotamus upon his death in the <a href="/wiki/Sankarani_River" title="Sankarani River">Sankarani River</a> and that it's possible to find villages in the area of this river, termed "old Mali", which have Mali for a name. A study of Malian proverbs noted that in old Mali, there is a village called Malikoma, which means "New Mali", and that <i>Mali</i> could have formerly been the name of a city.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another theory suggests that <i>Mali</i> is a <a href="/wiki/Fula_language" title="Fula language">Fulani</a> pronunciation of the name of the <a href="/wiki/Mand%C3%A9_peoples" title="Mandé peoples">Mande peoples</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> It is suggested that a sound shift led to the change, whereby in Fulani the alveolar segment <span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="und-Latn-fonipa">/nd/</span> shifts to <span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="und-Latn-fonipa">/l/</span> and the terminal vowel denasalizes and raises, leading "Manden" to shift to <span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="und-Latn-fonipa">/mali/</span>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:MALI_empire_map.PNG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/MALI_empire_map.PNG/220px-MALI_empire_map.PNG" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/MALI_empire_map.PNG/330px-MALI_empire_map.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/MALI_empire_map.PNG/440px-MALI_empire_map.PNG 2x" data-file-width="1580" data-file-height="994" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:MALI_empire_map.PNG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The extent of the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a>'s peak</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg/220px-Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg/330px-Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg/440px-Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="683" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Timbuktu-manuscripts-astronomy-mathematics.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The pages above are from <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu_Manuscripts" title="Timbuktu Manuscripts">Timbuktu Manuscripts</a> written in Sudani script (a form of <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a>) from the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> showing established knowledge of astronomy and mathematics. Today there are close to a million of these manuscripts found in <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a> alone.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:GriotsSambala.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/GriotsSambala.jpg/220px-GriotsSambala.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/GriotsSambala.jpg/330px-GriotsSambala.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/GriotsSambala.jpg/440px-GriotsSambala.jpg 2x" data-file-width="501" data-file-height="352" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:GriotsSambala.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Griot" title="Griot">Griots</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sambala" class="mw-redirect" title="Sambala">Sambala</a>, king of Médina (<a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula people</a>, Mali), 1890</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Mali" title="History of Mali">History of Mali</a></div>
<p>Rock paintings and carvings indicate that northern Mali has been inhabited since prehistoric times when the Sahara was fertile grassland. Farming took place by 5000 BC and iron was used around 500 BC.
</p><p>The rock art in the <a href="/wiki/Sahara" title="Sahara">Sahara</a> suggests that northern Mali has been inhabited since 10,000 BC, when the Sahara was fertile and rich in wildlife. Early ceramics have been discovered at the central Malian site of Ounjougou dating to about 9,400 BC, and are believed to represent an instance of the independent invention of pottery in the region.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the first millennium BC, early cities and towns were created by Mande peoples related to the <a href="/wiki/Soninke_people" title="Soninke people">Soninke people</a>, along the middle Niger River in central Mali, including <a href="/wiki/Dia,_Mali" title="Dia, Mali">Dia</a> which began from around 900 BC, and reached its peak around 600 BC,<sup id="cite_ref-Arazi_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Arazi-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Djenne-Djenno" class="mw-redirect" title="Djenne-Djenno">Djenne-Djenno</a>, which lasted from around 300 BC to 900 AD. By the sixth century AD, the lucrative trans-Saharan trade in gold, salt and slaves had begun, facilitating the rise of West Africa's great empires.
</p><p>There are a few references to Mali in early Islamic literature. Among these are references to "Pene" and "Malal" in the work of <a href="/wiki/Al-Bakri" title="Al-Bakri">al-Bakri</a> in 1068,<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> the story of the conversion of an early ruler, known to <a href="/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun" title="Ibn Khaldun">Ibn Khaldun</a> (by 1397) as Barmandana,<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> and a few geographical details in the work of <a href="/wiki/Al-Idrisi" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Idrisi">al-Idrisi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mali was once part of three famed West African empires which controlled <a href="/wiki/Trans-Saharan_trade" title="Trans-Saharan trade">trans-Saharan trade</a> in gold, salt, other precious commodities, and <a href="/wiki/Slaves" class="mw-redirect" title="Slaves">slaves</a> majorly during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Mansa_Musa" title="Mansa Musa">Mansa Musa</a> from c. 1312 – c. 1337.<sup id="cite_ref-p1_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p1-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> These <a href="/wiki/Sahelian_kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Sahelian kingdom">Sahelian kingdoms</a> had neither rigid geopolitical boundaries nor rigid ethnic identities.<sup id="cite_ref-p1_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p1-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The earliest of these empires was the <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Empire" title="Ghana Empire">Ghana Empire</a>, which was dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Soninke_people" title="Soninke people">Soninke</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Mande_languages" title="Mande languages">Mande</a>-speaking people.<sup id="cite_ref-p1_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p1-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The empire expanded throughout West Africa from the eighth century until 1078, when it was conquered by the <a href="/wiki/Almoravids" class="mw-redirect" title="Almoravids">Almoravids</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kirina" title="Battle of Kirina">Battle of Kirina</a> in 1235, culminated in a victory for the <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Mandinka</a> under the command of the exiled prince <a href="/wiki/Sundiata_Keita" title="Sundiata Keita">Sundiata Keita</a>, which led to the downfall of the <a href="/wiki/Sosso_Empire" title="Sosso Empire">Sosso Empire</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> later formed on the upper <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a>, and reached the height of power in the 14th century.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Under the Mali Empire, the ancient cities of <a href="/wiki/Djenn%C3%A9" title="Djenné">Djenné</a> and <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a> were centers of both trade and Islamic learning.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The empire later declined as a result of internal intrigue, ultimately being supplanted by the <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The Songhai people originated in current northwestern <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>. The Songhai had long been a major power in West Africa subject to the Mali Empire's rule.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the late 14th century, the Songhai gradually gained independence from the Mali Empire and expanded, ultimately subsuming the entire eastern portion of the Mali Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The Songhai Empire's eventual collapse was largely the result of a <a href="/wiki/Saadi_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Saadi dynasty">Moroccan</a> invasion in 1591, under the command of <a href="/wiki/Judar_Pasha" title="Judar Pasha">Judar Pasha</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The fall of the Songhai Empire marked the end of the region's role as a trading crossroads.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Following the <a href="/wiki/European_exploration_of_Africa" title="European exploration of Africa">establishment of sea routes by the European powers</a>, the trans-Saharan trade routes lost significance.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> At that time, the Mali Empire's abundance in wealth expanded its commercial assets of <a href="/wiki/Salt" title="Salt">salt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gold" title="Gold">gold</a>.
</p><p>One of the worst <a href="/wiki/Famine" title="Famine">famines</a> in the region's recorded history occurred in the 18th century. According to <a href="/wiki/John_Iliffe_(historian)" title="John Iliffe (historian)">John Iliffe</a>, "The worst crises were in the 1680s, when famine extended from the Senegambian coast to the Upper Nile and 'many sold themselves for slaves, only to get a sustenance', and especially in 1738–1756, when West Africa's greatest recorded subsistence crisis, due to drought and locusts, reportedly killed half the population of <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="French_colonial_rule">French colonial rule</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: French colonial rule">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg/170px-Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg/255px-Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg/340px-Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1605" data-file-height="1953" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Africa._French_West_Africa._Currently_the_most_important_efforts_of_the_Office_du_Niger_are_directed_toward_the..._-_NARA_-_541637.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Cotton being processed in <a href="/wiki/Niono" title="Niono">Niono</a> into 180&#160;kg (400&#160;lb) bales for export to other parts of Africa and to France, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1950</span></div></div></div>
<p>Mali fell under the control of France during the late 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> By 1905, most of the area was under firm French control as a part of <a href="/wiki/French_Sudan" title="French Sudan">French Sudan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> On 24 November 1958, French Sudan (which changed its name to the Sudanese Republic) became an autonomous republic within the <a href="/wiki/French_Community" title="French Community">French Community</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Independent_Mali_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Independent_Mali-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In January 1959, Mali and <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> united to become the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Federation" title="Mali Federation">Mali Federation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Independent_Mali_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Independent_Mali-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The Mali Federation gained independence from France on 20 June 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Senegal withdrew from the federation in August 1960, which allowed the Sudanese Republic to become the independent Republic of Mali on 22 September 1960, and that date is now the country's <a href="/wiki/List_of_national_independence_days" title="List of national independence days">Independence Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Modibo_Ke%C3%AFta" title="Modibo Keïta">Modibo Keïta</a> was elected the first president.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Keïta quickly established a one-party state, adopted an independent African and socialist orientation with close ties to the East, and implemented extensive nationalization of economic resources.<sup id="cite_ref-p2_37-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p2-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In 1960, the population of Mali was reported to be about 4.1&#160;million.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Moussa_Traor.C3.A9"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Moussa_Traoré">Moussa Traoré</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Moussa Traoré">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 19 November 1968, following progressive economic decline, the Keïta regime was overthrown in a bloodless military coup led by <a href="/wiki/Moussa_Traor%C3%A9" title="Moussa Traoré">Moussa Traoré</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> a day which is now commemorated as <a href="/wiki/Liberation_Day" title="Liberation Day">Liberation Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The subsequent military-led regime, with Traoré as president, attempted to reform the economy. His efforts were frustrated by political turmoil and a devastating <a href="/wiki/Sahel_drought" title="Sahel drought">drought</a> between 1968 and 1974,<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> in which famine killed thousands of people.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The Traoré regime faced student unrest beginning in the late 1970s and three coup attempts. The Traoré regime repressed all dissenters until the late 1980s.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government continued to attempt economic reforms, and the populace became increasingly dissatisfied.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> In response to growing demands for multi-party democracy, the Traoré regime allowed some limited political liberalization. They refused to usher in a full-fledged democratic system.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> In 1990, cohesive opposition movements began to emerge, and was complicated by the turbulent rise of ethnic violence in the north following the return of many <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuaregs</a> to Mali.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg/220px-Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg/330px-Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg/440px-Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Place_de_la_libert%C3%A9_-_Bamako.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>WWI Commemorative Monument to the "Armée Noire"</div></div></div>
<p>Anti-government protests in 1991 led to a coup, a transitional government, and a <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Mali" title="Constitution of Mali">new constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-p3_42-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p3-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Opposition to the corrupt and dictatorial regime of General Moussa Traoré grew during the 1980s. During this time strict programs, imposed to satisfy demands of the International Monetary Fund, brought increased hardship upon the country's population, while elites close to the government supposedly lived in growing wealth. Peaceful student protests in January 1991 were brutally suppressed, with mass arrests and torture of leaders and participants.<sup id="cite_ref-nonviolent_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nonviolent-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Scattered acts of rioting and vandalism of public buildings followed, but most actions by the dissidents remained nonviolent.<sup id="cite_ref-nonviolent_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nonviolent-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="March_Revolution">March Revolution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: March Revolution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>From 22 March through 26 March 1991, mass pro-democracy rallies and a nationwide strike was held in both urban and rural communities, which became known as <i>les évenements</i> ("the events") or the March Revolution. In Bamako, in response to mass demonstrations organized by university students and later joined by trade unionists and others, soldiers opened fire indiscriminately on the nonviolent demonstrators. Riots broke out briefly following the shootings. Barricades as well as roadblocks were erected and Traoré declared a state of emergency and imposed a nightly curfew. Despite an estimated loss of 300 lives over the course of four days, nonviolent protesters continued to return to Bamako each day demanding the resignation of the dictatorial president and the implementation of democratic policies.<sup id="cite_ref-nesbitt_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nesbitt-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>26 March 1991 is the day that marks the clash between military soldiers and peaceful demonstrating students which climaxed in the massacre of dozens under the orders of then President Moussa Traoré. He and three associates were later tried and convicted and received the death sentence for their part in the decision-making of that day. Nowadays, the day is a national holiday in order to remember the tragic events and the people who were killed.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (July 2013)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The coup is remembered as Mali's March Revolution of 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 26 March, the growing refusal of soldiers to fire into the largely nonviolent protesting crowds turned into a full-scale tumult, and resulted in thousands of soldiers putting down their arms and joining the pro-democracy movement. That afternoon, Lieutenant Colonel <a href="/wiki/Amadou_Toumani_Tour%C3%A9" title="Amadou Toumani Touré">Amadou Toumani Touré</a> announced on the radio that he had arrested the dictatorial president, Moussa Traoré. As a consequence, opposition parties were legalized and a national congress of civil and political groups met to draft a new democratic constitution to be approved by a national referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-nesbitt_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nesbitt-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Amadou_Toumani_Tour.C3.A9_presidency"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Amadou_Toumani_Touré_presidency">Amadou Toumani Touré presidency</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Amadou Toumani Touré presidency">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1992, <a href="/wiki/Alpha_Oumar_Konar%C3%A9" title="Alpha Oumar Konaré">Alpha Oumar Konaré</a> won Mali's first democratic, multi-party presidential election, before being re-elected for a second term in 1997, which was the last allowed under the constitution. <a href="/wiki/Amadou_Toumani_Tour%C3%A9" title="Amadou Toumani Touré">Amadou Toumani Touré</a>, a retired general who had been the leader of the military aspect of the 1991 democratic uprising, was elected in 2002.<sup id="cite_ref-p4_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-p4-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> During this democratic period Mali was regarded as one of the most politically and socially stable countries in Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_modern_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Slavery in modern Africa">Slavery</a> persists in Mali today with as many as 200,000 people held in direct servitude to a master.<sup id="cite_ref-slaves_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-slaves-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_Rebellion_(2012)" class="mw-redirect" title="Tuareg Rebellion (2012)">Tuareg Rebellion of 2012</a>, ex-slaves were a vulnerable population with reports of some slaves being recaptured by their former masters.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Northern_Mali_conflict">Northern Mali conflict</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Northern Mali conflict">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mali_War" title="Mali War">Mali War</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_(6904946068).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg/220px-Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg/330px-Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg/440px-Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_%286904946068%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Le_Mali_confront%C3%A9_aux_sanctions_et_%C3%A0_lavanc%C3%A9e_des_rebelles_islamistes_(6904946068).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Tuareg separatist rebels in Mali, January 2012</div></div></div>
<p>In January 2012 <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_rebellion_(2012)" title="Tuareg rebellion (2012)">a Tuareg rebellion</a> began in Northern Mali, led by the <a href="/wiki/National_Movement_for_the_Liberation_of_Azawad" title="National Movement for the Liberation of Azawad">National Movement for the Liberation of Azawad</a> (MNLA).<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In March, military officer <a href="/wiki/Amadou_Sanogo" title="Amadou Sanogo">Amadou Sanogo</a> seized power in <a href="/wiki/2012_Malian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2012 Malian coup d&#39;état">a coup d'état</a>, citing Touré's failures in quelling the rebellion, and leading to sanctions and an embargo by the <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> The MNLA quickly took control of the north, declaring independence as <a href="/wiki/Azawad" title="Azawad">Azawad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> However, Islamist groups including <a href="/wiki/Ansar_Dine" title="Ansar Dine">Ansar Dine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb" title="Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb">Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb (AQIM)</a>, who had helped the MNLA defeat the government, turned on the Tuareg and took control of the North<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> with the goal of implementing <a href="/wiki/Sharia" title="Sharia">sharia</a> in Mali.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 January 2013, the <a href="/wiki/French_Armed_Forces" title="French Armed Forces">French Armed Forces</a> <a href="/wiki/Operation_Serval" title="Operation Serval">intervened</a> at the request of the interim government.
On 30 January, the coordinated advance of the French and Malian troops claimed to have retaken the last remaining Islamist stronghold of Kidal, which was also the last of three northern provincial capitals.<sup id="cite_ref-kidal_retaken_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kidal_retaken-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> On 2 February, French president <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande" title="François Hollande">François Hollande</a> joined Mali's interim president <a href="/wiki/Dioncounda_Traor%C3%A9" title="Dioncounda Traoré">Dioncounda Traoré</a> in a public appearance in recently recaptured Timbuktu.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC-Hollande_Timbuktu_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-Hollande_Timbuktu-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 2013, <a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Boubacar_Keita" class="mw-redirect" title="Ibrahim Boubacar Keita">Ibrahim Boubacar Keita</a> was elected as the new <a href="/wiki/President_of_Mali" class="mw-redirect" title="President of Mali">president of Mali</a> after his rival admitted defeat in the second round of the <a href="/wiki/2013_Malian_presidential_election" title="2013 Malian presidential election">election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conflict_in_Central_Mali">Conflict in Central Mali</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Conflict in Central Mali">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the central Mali province of <a href="/wiki/Mopti_Region" title="Mopti Region">Mopti</a>, conflict has escalated since 2015 between agricultural communities like the <a href="/wiki/Dogon_people" title="Dogon people">Dogon</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bambara_people" title="Bambara people">Bambara</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Pastoralism" title="Pastoralism">pastoral</a> <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula (or Fulani) people</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Historically, the two sides have fought over access to land and water, factors which have been exacerbated by <a href="/wiki/Climate_change" title="Climate change">climate change</a> as the Fula move into new areas.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The Dogon and the Bambara communities have formed militias, or "self-defense groups",<sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> to fight the Fula. They accuse the Fula of working with armed <a href="/wiki/Islamic_terrorism" title="Islamic terrorism">Islamists</a> linked to <a href="/wiki/Al-Qaeda" title="Al-Qaeda">al-Qaeda</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> While some Fula have joined Islamist groups, <a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a> reports that the links have been "exaggerated and instrumentalized by different actors for opportunistic ends".<sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Added a top Mali military commander:</p><blockquote><p>"I’ve discussed the growing violence with my commanders and with village chiefs from all sides. Yes, sure, there are jihadists in this zone, but the real problem is banditry, animal theft, score settling – people are enriching themselves using the fight against terrorists as a cover."<sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>The conflict has seen the creation and growth of Dogon and Bambara militias. The government of Mali is suspected of supporting some of these groups under the guise of they being proxies in the war against Islamists in the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Mali_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Mali conflict">Northern Mali conflict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:5_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> The government denies this.<sup id="cite_ref-:5_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> One such militia is the Dogon group <a href="/wiki/Dan_Na_Ambassagou" title="Dan Na Ambassagou">Dan Na Ambassagou</a>, created in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_63-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2018_elections">2018 elections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: 2018 elections">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/2018_Malian_presidential_election" title="2018 Malian presidential election">2018 Malian presidential election</a></div>
<p>Presidential elections were held in Mali on 29 July 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In July 2018, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Constitutional_Court_of_Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Constitutional Court of Mali (page does not exist)">Constitutional Court</a> approved the nomination of a total of 24 candidates in the election.<sup id="cite_ref-africanews_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-africanews-68">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> As no candidate received more than 50% of the vote in the first round, a runoff was held on 12 August 2018 between the top two candidates, incumbent president <a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Boubacar_Ke%C3%AFta" title="Ibrahim Boubacar Keïta">Ibrahim Boubacar Keïta</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Rally_for_Mali" title="Rally for Mali">Rally for Mali</a> and <a href="/wiki/Souma%C3%AFla_Ciss%C3%A9" title="Soumaïla Cissé">Soumaïla Cissé</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Union_for_the_Republic_and_Democracy" title="Union for the Republic and Democracy">Union for the Republic and Democracy</a>. Keïta was subsequently re-elected with 67% of the vote.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2018_ceasefire_and_aftermath">2018 ceasefire and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: 2018 ceasefire and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In September 2018, the <a href="/wiki/Centre_for_Humanitarian_Dialogue" title="Centre for Humanitarian Dialogue">Centre for Humanitarian Dialogue</a> negotiated a unilateral ceasefire with Dan Na Ambassagou "in the context of the conflict which opposes the group to other community armed groups in central Mali".<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> However, the group has been blamed for the <a href="/wiki/March_2019_attacks_against_Fulani_herders" class="mw-redirect" title="March 2019 attacks against Fulani herders">24 March 2019 massacre of 160 Fula villagers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The group denied the attack, but afterwards Malian president Keita ordered the group to disband.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The UN Special Adviser on the Prevention of Genocide, <a href="/wiki/Adama_Dieng" title="Adama Dieng">Adama Dieng</a>, warned of a growing <a href="/wiki/Ethnicization" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnicization">ethnicization</a> of the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The United Nations reported that the number of children killed in the conflict in the first six months of 2019 was twice as many for the entire year of 2018. Many of the children have been killed in intercommunal attacks attributed to ethnic militias, with the majority of attacks occurring around <a href="/wiki/Mopti" title="Mopti">Mopti</a>. It is reported that around 900 schools have closed down and that armed militias are recruiting children.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the first week of October 2019, two jihadist attacks in the towns of Boulikessi and <a href="/wiki/Mondoro" title="Mondoro">Mondoro</a> killed more than 25 Mali soldiers near the border with <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> President Keïta declared that "no military coup will prevail in Mali", continuing by saying that he doesn't think it "is on the agenda at all and cannot worry us".<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="2020_coup_d.27.C3.A9tat_and_aftermath"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="2020_coup_d'état_and_aftermath">2020 coup d'état and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 2020 coup d&#039;état and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2020_Malian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2020 Malian coup d&#39;état">2020 Malian coup d'état</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg/220px-Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg/330px-Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg/440px-Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="540" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Comit%C3%A9_national_pour_le_salut_du_Peuple_-_2020_Malian_coup_d%27Etat_2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Members of the <a href="/wiki/National_Committee_for_the_Salvation_of_the_People" class="mw-redirect" title="National Committee for the Salvation of the People">National Committee for the Salvation of the People</a>, directory of the ruling junta in Mali.</div></div></div>
<p>Popular unrest began on 5 June 2020 following irregularities in the March and April parliamentary elections, including outrage against the kidnapping of opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Souma%C3%AFla_Ciss%C3%A9" title="Soumaïla Cissé">Soumaïla Cissé</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Between 11 and 23 deaths followed protests that took place from 10 to 13 June.<sup id="cite_ref-cnews_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnews-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> In July, President Keïta dissolved the constitutional court.
</p><p>Members of the military led by Colonel <a href="/wiki/Assimi_Go%C3%AFta" title="Assimi Goïta">Assimi Goïta</a> and Colonel-Major <a href="/wiki/Isma%C3%ABl_Wagu%C3%A9" title="Ismaël Wagué">Ismaël Wagué</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kati,_Mali" title="Kati, Mali">Kati</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koulikoro_Region" title="Koulikoro Region">Koulikoro Region</a>, began a mutiny on 18 August 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-cnews_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnews-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> President Ibrahim Boubacar Keïta and Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Boubou_Ciss%C3%A9" title="Boubou Cissé">Boubou Cissé</a> were arrested, and shortly after midnight Keïta announced his resignation, saying he did not want to see any bloodshed.<sup id="cite_ref-cnews_79-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnews-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Wagué announced the formation of the National Committee for the Salvation of the People (CNSP) and promised elections in the future. A curfew was begun and the streets of Bamako were quiet.<sup id="cite_ref-cnews_79-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnews-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a> (ECOWAS) condemned the coup and demanded the reinstallation of President Keïta.<sup id="cite_ref-neighbours_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-neighbours-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 September 2020, the <a href="/wiki/National_Committee_for_the_Salvation_of_the_People" class="mw-redirect" title="National Committee for the Salvation of the People">National Committee for the Salvation of the People</a> (CNSP) agreed to an 18-month political transition to civilian rule. Shortly after, <a href="/wiki/Bah_Ndaw" title="Bah Ndaw">Bah N'daw</a> was named interim president by a group of 17 electors, with Goïta being appointed vice president. The government was inaugurated on 25 September 2020.
</p><p>On 18 January 2021, the transitional government announced that the CNSP had been disbanded, almost four months after had been promised under the initial agreement.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="2021_coup_d.27.C3.A9tat"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="2021_coup_d'état">2021 coup d'état</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 2021 coup d&#039;état">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2021_Malian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2021 Malian coup d&#39;état">2021 Malian coup d'état</a></div>
<p>Tensions have been high between the civilian transitional government and the military since the handover of power in September 2020.
</p><p>On 24 May, tensions came to a head after a cabinet reshuffle, where two leaders of the 2020 military coup – <a href="/wiki/Sadio_Camara" title="Sadio Camara">Sadio Camara</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Modibo_Kone&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Modibo Kone (page does not exist)">Modibo Kone</a> – were replaced by N'daw's administration.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Later that day, journalists reported that three key civilian leaders – President N'daw, Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Moctar_Ouane" title="Moctar Ouane">Moctar Ouane</a> and Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Souleymane_Doucour%C3%A9" title="Souleymane Doucouré">Souleymane Doucouré</a>, were being detained in a military base in <a href="/wiki/Kati,_Mali" title="Kati, Mali">Kati</a>, outside Bamako.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> On 7 June 2021, Mali's military commander <a href="/wiki/Assimi_Goita" class="mw-redirect" title="Assimi Goita">Assimi Goita</a> was sworn into office as the new interim president.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2022">2022</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: 2022">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 10 January, Mali announced the closure of its borders and recalled several ambassadors to <a href="/wiki/ECOWAS" class="mw-redirect" title="ECOWAS">ECOWAS</a> countries, in response to sanctions placed on Mali for deferring elections for four years.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> On 4 February, France's ambassador was expelled.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a> (HRW) Malian troops and suspected Russian mercenaries from the <a href="/wiki/Wagner_Group" title="Wagner Group">Wagner group</a> executed around 300 civilian men in central Mali in March 2022. France had withdrawn French troops from Mali in February 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> On 2 May, the military government announced breaking its defence accords concluded in 2013 with France, constituting an additional step in the deterioration of Malian-French relations.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> This latest announcement has been criticized by French authorities and considered as "illegitimate".<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> A UN panel reported that in the first three months of 2022, 543 civilians were killed and 269 wounded, warning the 2015 peace agreement between the government and pro-independence groups was threatened by a potential risk of confrontation for the first time in five years. The report also noted a sharp increase in the number of people needing humanitarian assistance over the previous year.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2023">2023</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: 2023">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Sergey Lavrov, the Russian foreign minister, visited Bamako, Mali, on Tuesday February 7 and said that Moscow would continue to help Bamako improve its military capabilities.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> On February 23, Mali became one of seven countries to vote against the UN General Assembly motion calling for Russia to withdraw from Ukraine.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mali_sat.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Mali_sat.png/220px-Mali_sat.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="198" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Mali_sat.png/330px-Mali_sat.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Mali_sat.png/440px-Mali_sat.png 2x" data-file-width="1979" data-file-height="1783" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mali_sat.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Satellite image of Mali</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg/220px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg/330px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg/440px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1218" data-file-height="850" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_MLI_present.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Mali map of Köppen climate classification</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hand_der_Fatima.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Hand_der_Fatima.jpg/220px-Hand_der_Fatima.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Hand_der_Fatima.jpg/330px-Hand_der_Fatima.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Hand_der_Fatima.jpg/440px-Hand_der_Fatima.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hand_der_Fatima.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Landscape in <a href="/wiki/Hombori" title="Hombori">Hombori</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Mali" title="Geography of Mali">Geography of Mali</a></div>
<p>Mali is a landlocked country in West Africa, located southwest of <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a>. It lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/10th_parallel_north" title="10th parallel north">10°</a> and <a href="/wiki/25th_parallel_north" title="25th parallel north">25°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/13th_meridian_west" title="13th meridian west">13°W</a> and <a href="/wiki/5th_meridian_east" title="5th meridian east">5°E</a>. Mali borders <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> to <a href="/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Mali_border" title="Algeria–Mali border">the north-northeast</a>, <a href="/wiki/Niger" title="Niger">Niger</a> to <a href="/wiki/Mali%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Mali–Niger border">the east</a>, <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> to <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso%E2%80%93Mali_border" title="Burkina Faso–Mali border">the south-east</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a> to <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast%E2%80%93Mali_border" title="Ivory Coast–Mali border">the south</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guinea" title="Guinea">Guinea</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Mali_border" title="Guinea–Mali border">the south-west</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> to <a href="/wiki/Mali%E2%80%93Senegal_border" title="Mali–Senegal border">the west</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mauritania" title="Mauritania">Mauritania</a> to <a href="/wiki/Mali%E2%80%93Mauritania_border" title="Mali–Mauritania border">the north-west</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At 1,242,248 square kilometres (479,635&#160;sq&#160;mi), Mali is the world's <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_outlying_territories_by_total_area" class="mw-redirect" title="List of countries and outlying territories by total area">24th-largest country</a> and is comparable in size to South Africa or <a href="/wiki/Angola" title="Angola">Angola</a>. Most of the country lies in the southern <a href="/wiki/Sahara_Desert" class="mw-redirect" title="Sahara Desert">Sahara Desert</a>, which produces an extremely hot, dust-laden <a href="/wiki/Sudan_(region)" title="Sudan (region)">Sudanian savanna</a> zone.<sup id="cite_ref-geography_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-geography-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Mali is mostly flat, rising to rolling northern plains covered by <a href="/wiki/Sand" title="Sand">sand</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Adrar_des_Ifoghas" title="Adrar des Ifoghas">Adrar des Ifoghas</a> massif lies in the northeast.
</p><p>Mali lies in the <a href="/wiki/Geographical_zone#Torrid_Zone" title="Geographical zone">torrid zone</a> and is among the hottest countries in the world. The <a href="/wiki/Thermal_equator" title="Thermal equator">thermal equator</a>, which matches the hottest spots year-round on the planet based on the mean daily annual temperature, crosses the country.<sup id="cite_ref-geography_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-geography-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Most of Mali receives negligible rainfall and droughts are very frequent.<sup id="cite_ref-geography_93-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-geography-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Late April to early October is the rainy season in the southernmost area. During this time, flooding of the Niger River is common, creating the <a href="/wiki/Inner_Niger_Delta" title="Inner Niger Delta">Inner Niger Delta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-geography_93-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-geography-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> The vast northern desert part of Mali has a <a href="/wiki/Hot_desert_climate" class="mw-redirect" title="Hot desert climate">hot desert climate</a> (<a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Köppen climate classification</a> <i>BWh</i>) with long, extremely hot summers and scarce rainfall which decreases northwards. The central area has a <a href="/wiki/Hot_semi-arid_climate" class="mw-redirect" title="Hot semi-arid climate">hot semi-arid climate</a> (<a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Köppen climate classification</a> <i>BSh</i>) with very high temperatures year-round, a long, intense dry season and a brief, irregular rainy season. The southern areas have a <a href="/wiki/Tropical_wet_and_dry_climate" class="mw-redirect" title="Tropical wet and dry climate">tropical wet and dry climate</a>. (<a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Köppen climate classification</a> <i>Aw</i>) In review, Mali's climate is tropical, with March to May being the hot, dry season. June to October is rainy, humid and mild. November to February is the cool, dry season.
</p><p>Mali has considerable natural resources, with gold, uranium, <a href="/wiki/Phosphates" class="mw-redirect" title="Phosphates">phosphates</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kaolinite" title="Kaolinite">kaolinite</a>, salt and <a href="/wiki/Limestone" title="Limestone">limestone</a> being most widely exploited. Mali is estimated to have in excess of 17,400 tonnes of uranium (measured + indicated + inferred).<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In 2012, a further uranium mineralized north zone was identified.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Mali faces numerous environmental challenges, including <a href="/wiki/Desertification" title="Desertification">desertification</a>, <a href="/wiki/Deforestation" title="Deforestation">deforestation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Soil_erosion" title="Soil erosion">soil erosion</a>, and inadequate <a href="/wiki/Water_supply" title="Water supply">supplies</a> of <a href="/wiki/Potable_water" class="mw-redirect" title="Potable water">potable water</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-geography_93-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-geography-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Five terrestrial ecoregions lie within Mali's borders: <a href="/wiki/Sahelian_Acacia_savanna" class="mw-redirect" title="Sahelian Acacia savanna">Sahelian Acacia savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna" title="West Sudanian savanna">West Sudanian savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Inner_Niger_Delta_flooded_savanna" class="mw-redirect" title="Inner Niger Delta flooded savanna">Inner Niger Delta flooded savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Saharan_steppe_and_woodlands" title="South Saharan steppe and woodlands">South Saharan steppe and woodlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Saharan_montane_xeric_woodlands" title="West Saharan montane xeric woodlands">West Saharan montane xeric woodlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The country had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 7.16/10, ranking it 51st globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:right;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Regions_and_cercles">Regions and cercles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mali&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Regions and cercles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Mali" title="Regions of Mali">Regions of Mali</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cercles_of_Mali" title="Cercles of Mali">Cercles of Mali</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Communes_of_Mali" title="Communes of Mali">Communes of Mali</a></div>
<div class="floatright"><div class="noresize" style="height: 355px; width: 360px; "><map name="ImageMap_1c9fd35f5b5256f2"><area href="/wiki/Tombouctou_Region" shape="rect" coords="139,113,239,150" alt="Tombouctou Region" title="Tombouctou Region" /><area href="/wiki/Kidal_Region" shape="rect" coords="264,110,309,141" alt="Kidal Region" title="Kidal Region" /><area href="/wiki/Gao_Region" shape="rect" coords="268,176,308,204" alt="Gao Region" title="Gao Region" /><area href="/wiki/Mopti_Region" shape="rect" coords="164,220,219,247" alt="Mopti Region" title="Mopti Region" /><area href="/wiki/Koulikoro_Region" shape="rect" coords="69,176,147,203" alt="Koulikoro Region" title="Koulikoro Region" /><area href="/wiki/Kayes_Region" shape="rect" coords="10,232,67,264" alt="Kayes Region" title="Kayes Region" /><area href="/wiki/Bamako" shape="rect" coords="6,301,75,329" alt="Bamako" title="Bamako" /><area href="/wiki/Bamako" shape="rect" coords="88,277,100,289" alt="Bamako" title="Bamako" /><area href="/wiki/Sikasso" shape="rect" coords="89,305,150,329" alt="Sikasso" title="Sikasso" /><area href="/wiki/S%C3%A9gou_Region" shape="rect" coords="121,257,179,281" alt="Ségou Region" title="Ségou Region" /></map><img alt="A clickable map of Mali exhibiting its eight regions and capital district." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/Mali_Regions.png/360px-Mali_Regions.png" decoding="async" width="360" height="355" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/08/Mali_Regions.png 1.5x" data-file-width="449" data-file-height="443" usemap="#ImageMap_1c9fd35f5b5256f2" /><div style="margin-left: 0px; margin-top: -20px; text-align: left;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mali_Regions.png" title="About this image"><img alt="About this image" src="/w/extensions/ImageMap/resources/desc-20.png?15600" style="border: none;" /></a></div></div></div>
<p>Since 2016, Mali has been divided into ten regions and the District of Bamako.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Each region has a governor.<sup id="cite_ref-pictures_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pictures-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> The implementation of the two newest regions, Taoudénit (formerly part of Tombouctou Region) and Ménaka (formerly <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9naka_Cercle" title="Ménaka Cercle">Ménaka Cercle</a> in Gao Region), has been ongoing since January 2016;<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-malijet_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-malijet-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> a governor and transitional council has been appointed for both regions.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> The ten regions in turn are subdivided into 56 <a href="/wiki/Cercle_(Mali)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cercle (Mali)"><i>cercle</i>s</a> and 703 <a href="/wiki/Communes_of_Mali" title="Communes of Mali"><i>communes</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Mali" title="Regions of Mali"><i>régions</i></a> and Capital District are:
</p>
